This chapter covers the fundamental building blocks of algebra, focusing on the number system, manipulation of expressions, and simplification techniques.

## Key Mathematical Concepts

### 1. The Real Number System
Understanding the hierarchy of numbers, including natural numbers, integers, rational numbers, and irrational numbers.

### 2. Rational and Irrational Numbers
*   **Rational numbers:** Numbers that can be expressed as a fraction $\frac{a}{b}$ where $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $b \neq 0$.
*   **Irrational numbers:** Numbers that cannot be expressed as a simple fraction, such as $\sqrt{2}$ or $\pi$.

### 3. Surds
Expressions involving roots that cannot be simplified into rational numbers. Estimating these involves finding the nearest perfect squares:
$$\sqrt{n} \approx \text{estimate}$$

### 4. Algebraic Manipulation
*   **Products:** Expanding expressions such as $(x + a)(x + b) = x^2 + (a+b)x + ab$.
*   **Factorisation:** The reverse process of expanding, breaking expressions down into their prime factors.
*   **Simplification of Fractions:** Reducing algebraic fractions by cancelling common factors:
$$\frac{ax + ay}{a} = x + y \quad (a \neq 0)$$

# 1. Algebraic Expressions

## 1.1 Introduction
Mathematics is a universal language developed over centuries. While algebra may seem abstract, it is foundational to modern life. This chapter focuses on:
*   Revising the real number system.
*   Estimating surds.
*   Rounding real numbers.
*   Expanding on factorisation.
*   Performing calculations with binomial and trinomial expressions.

## 1.2 The Real Number System

The real number system ($\mathbb{R}$) is composed of rational ($\mathbb{Q}$) and irrational ($\mathbb{Q}'$) numbers. The rational numbers further contain integers, whole numbers, and natural numbers.

<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->

### Definitions
*   **Natural numbers ($\mathbb{N}$):** The set of counting numbers:
    $$\mathbb{N} = \{1; 2; 3; \dots\}$$
*   **Whole numbers ($\mathbb{N}_0$):** The set of natural numbers including zero:
    $$\mathbb{N}_0 = \{0; 1; 2; 3; \dots\}$$
*   **Integers ($\mathbb{Z}$):** The set of all whole numbers and their negative counterparts:
    $$\mathbb{Z} = \{\dots; -3; -2; -1; 0; 1; 2; 3; \dots\}$$

---

### 1.3 Rational and Irrational Numbers

#### Rational Numbers ($\mathbb{Q}$)
A rational number is defined as any number that can be expressed in the form:
$$\frac{a}{b}$$
Where:
*   $a$ and $b$ are integers.
*   $b \neq 0$ (division by zero is undefined).

**Examples of rational numbers:**
$$\frac{10}{1}, \frac{21}{7}, \frac{-1}{-3}, \frac{10}{20}, \frac{-3}{6}$$

*Note: All integers are rational numbers because they can be written as a fraction with a denominator of $1$.*

#### Irrational Numbers ($\mathbb{Q}'$)
Irrational numbers are numbers that **cannot** be written as a fraction where both the numerator and denominator are integers.

**Examples of irrational numbers:**
$$\sqrt{2}, \sqrt{3}, \sqrt[3]{4}, \pi, \frac{1 + \sqrt{5}}{2}$$

---

### Decimal Numbers

All integers and fractions with integer numerators and non-zero integer denominators are rational. These can be expressed as decimal numbers.

#### Types of Rational Decimals
1.  **Terminating decimals:** Decimals that end.
    *   Example: $\frac{4}{10} = 0,4$
2.  **Repeating (recurring) single digits:** Decimals where a single digit repeats infinitely.
    *   Example: $\frac{1}{3} = 0,\dot{3} = 0,\overline{3} = 0,333...$
3.  **Repeating patterns of multiple digits:** Decimals where a sequence of digits repeats infinitely.
    *   Example: $\frac{2}{11} = 0,\overline{18} = 0,181818...$

*Note: In many regions, a comma ($,$) is used as a decimal separator, but a full stop ($.$) is also commonly used (e.g., $0,4$ is the same as $0.4$).*

---

### Important Concepts: Non-Real Numbers
Not all numbers are real numbers. If you attempt to find the square root of a negative number, the result is classified as a **non-real** or **imaginary** number.

**Examples of non-real numbers:**
$$\sqrt{-1}, \sqrt{-28}, \sqrt{-5}$$

### Notation for Recurring Decimals
To indicate that a decimal is recurring (repeating), you can use a dot or a bar over the repeated digits. If a bar covers more than one digit, all numbers beneath the bar are recurring.

### Identifying Rational and Irrational Numbers
To determine if a number is rational or irrational, follow these steps:
1. Write the number in decimal form.
2. If the decimal **terminates** (ends), it is **rational**.
3. If the decimal goes on forever, check for a **repeated pattern** of digits.
    * If there is a repeated pattern, it is **rational**.
    * If there is no repeated pattern, it is **irrational**.

> **NOTE:** Rounding off an irrational number creates a rational number that acts as an approximation of the original irrational number.

---

### Worked Example 1: Rational and Irrational Numbers

**Question:** Which of the following are not rational numbers?
1. $\pi = 3,14159265358979323846264338327950288419716939937510...$
2. $1,4$
3. $1,618033989...$
4. $100$
5. $1,7373737373...$
6. $0,\overline{02}$

**Solution:**
1. **Irrational:** The decimal does not terminate and has no repeated pattern.
2. **Rational:** The decimal terminates.
3. **Irrational:** The decimal does not terminate and has no repeated pattern.
4. **Rational:** All integers are rational.
5. **Rational:** The decimal has a repeated pattern.
6. **Rational:** The decimal has a repeated pattern.

---

### Converting Terminating Decimals into Rational Numbers

A decimal number consists of an integer part and a fractional part. For example, $10,589$ has an integer part of $10$ and a fractional part of $0,589$, because:
$$10 + 0,589 = 10,589$$

Each digit after the decimal point represents a fraction with a denominator in increasing powers of $10$:

* $0,1 = \frac{1}{10}$
* $0,01 = \frac{1}{100}$
* $0,001 = \frac{1}{1000}$

![Textbook_Mathematics_Gr10_Number-patterns.pdf-0003-20.png](images/Textbook_Mathematics_Gr10_Number-patterns.pdf-0003-20.png)


---

### Converting Decimals to Fractions

To express a terminating decimal as a fraction, you can break it down into its place value components. For example, $10,589$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
10,589 &= 10 + \frac{5}{10} + \frac{8}{100} + \frac{9}{1000} \\
&= \frac{10\,000}{1000} + \frac{500}{1000} + \frac{80}{1000} + \frac{9}{1000} \\
&= \frac{10\,589}{1000}
\end{aligned}
$$

---

### Converting Recurring Decimals into Rational Numbers

When a decimal is a recurring decimal (a decimal that repeats infinitely), algebraic steps are required to convert it into the form $\frac{a}{b}$, where $a$ and $b$ are integers.

#### Worked Example: Converting $0,\dot{3}$ to a fraction

**Question:** Write $0,\dot{3}$ in the form $\frac{a}{b}$.

**Solution:**

**Step 1: Define an equation**
Let $x = 0,33333...$

**Step 2: Multiply by 10 on both sides**
To shift the decimal point past the first repeating digit, multiply by $10$:
$10x = 3,33333...$

**Step 3: Subtract the first equation from the second equation**
Subtract the original equation ($x = 0,33333...$) from the new equation ($10x = 3,33333...$):
$10x - x = 3,33333... - 0,33333...$
$9x = 3$

**Step 4: Simplify**
Solve for $x$ by dividing both sides by $9$:
$x = \frac{3}{9} = \frac{1}{3}$

### Worked Example 3: Converting Decimal Numbers to Fractions

**Question:**
Write $5,\dot{4}3\dot{2}$ as a rational fraction.

**Solution:**

*   **Step 1: Define an equation**
    $$x = 5,432432432...$$

*   **Step 2: Multiply by 1000 on both sides**
    $$1000x = 5432,432432432...$$

*   **Step 3: Subtract the first equation from the second equation**
    $$999x = 5427$$

*   **Step 4: Simplify**
    $$x = \frac{5427}{999} = \frac{201}{37} = 5\frac{16}{37}$$

---

### Key Concepts: Converting Recurring Decimals

*   **Rule for Multipliers:**
    *   If one digit is recurring, multiply by $10$.
    *   If two digits are recurring, multiply by $100$.
    *   If three digits are recurring, multiply by $1000$, and so on.

*   **Rational vs. Irrational Numbers:**
    *   **Rational numbers** can be written as a fraction of two integers.
    *   **Irrational numbers** (e.g., $\sqrt{2} = 1,4142135...$) cannot be written as a fraction because they do not terminate and do not have a repeating pattern of digits.

### 1. Number Systems and Sets

#### Conceptual Definitions
*   **Natural Numbers ($\mathbb{N}$):** The set of counting numbers $\{1, 2, 3, ...\}$.
*   **Whole Numbers ($\mathbb{N}_0$):** The set of natural numbers including zero $\{0, 1, 2, 3, ...\}$.
*   **Integers ($\mathbb{Z}$):** The set of all whole numbers and their negatives $\{..., -3, -2, -1, 0, 1, 2, 3, ...\}$.
*   **Rational Numbers:** Numbers that can be expressed as a fraction $\frac{a}{b}$ where $a$ and $b$ are integers and $b \neq 0$.
*   **Irrational Numbers:** Numbers that cannot be expressed as a simple fraction; their decimal expansions are non-terminating and non-recurring.
*   **Real Numbers:** The set of all rational and irrational numbers.
*   **Non-real Numbers:** Numbers that do not exist on the real number line (e.g., square roots of negative numbers).

### 1.4 Rounding off

Rounding off a decimal number to a given number of decimal places is the quickest way to approximate a number.

#### Rules for Rounding Off
To round off a number (e.g., $2,6525272$) to three decimal places:
1.  Count three places after the decimal and place a $|$ between the third and fourth numbers.
2.  **Round up:** If the fourth digit is greater than or equal to $5$.
3.  **Keep unchanged:** If the fourth digit is less than $5$.
4.  **Special case:** If the third digit is $9$ and needs to be rounded up, the $9$ becomes a $0$ and the second digit is rounded up.

**Worked Example:**
Round $2,6525272$ to three decimal places.
*   Identify the first three digits: $2,652$
*   Identify the fourth digit: $5$
*   Since the fourth digit is $5$, we round up the third digit ($2$ becomes $3$).
*   **Final Answer:** $2,653$

### Worked Example: Rounding Off

#### The Process of Rounding Off
To round off a number to a specific number of decimal places, follow these three steps:

**Step 1: Mark off the required number of decimal places**
If the number is not already a decimal, convert it to one first. Place a vertical line after the digit in the required decimal position.

**Step 2: Check the next digit**
Look at the first digit to the right of your vertical line:
*   If the digit is **less than 5**, round down (keep the last digit as it is).
*   If the digit is **5 or greater**, round up (increase the last digit by 1).

**Step 3: Write the final answer**
Remove all digits to the right of the vertical line.

---

#### Worked Example 4
**Question:** Round off the following numbers to the indicated number of decimal places:
1. $\frac{120}{99}$ to 3 decimal places.
2. $\pi = 3,141592653...$ to 4 decimal places.
3. $\sqrt{3} = 1,7320508...$ to 4 decimal places.
4. $2,78974526$ to 3 decimal places.

**Solution:**

| Number | Step 1: Mark off | Step 2: Check digit | Step 3: Final Answer |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1. $\frac{120}{99}$ | $1,212\|1212...$ | $1 < 5$ (Round down) | $1,212$ |
| 2. $\pi$ | $3,1415\|92653...$ | $9 \geq 5$ (Round up) | $3,1416$ |
| 3. $\sqrt{3}$ | $1,7320\|508...$ | $5 \geq 5$ (Round up) | $1,7321$ |
| 4. $2,7897...$ | $2,789\|74526$ | $7 \geq 5$ (Round up) | $2,790$ |

*Note on example 4: Since the digit after the line is 7, we round up. Because the digit in the 3rd decimal place is a 9, rounding up causes it to become 0 and increases the 2nd decimal place by 1.*

## 1.5 Estimating Surds

### Definition
If the $n^{\text{th}}$ root of a number cannot be simplified to a rational number, it is called a **surd**.
*   **Examples of surds:** $\sqrt{2}$, $\sqrt[3]{6}$, $\sqrt{5}$.
*   **Not a surd:** $\sqrt{4} = 2$ (because it simplifies to a rational number).

### Notation
We look at surds of the form $\sqrt[n]{a}$ where $a$ is any positive number.
*   When $n = 2$, we usually omit the index and write $\sqrt{a}$ instead of $\sqrt[2]{a}$.

### Estimating Surds without a Calculator
To estimate the position of a surd on a number line (e.g., $\sqrt{18}$), use the following property:

If $a$ and $b$ are positive whole numbers, and $a < b$, then:
$$\sqrt[n]{a} < \sqrt[n]{b}$$

This allows you to bound a surd between two perfect roots to determine its approximate value.

![Textbook_Mathematics_Gr10_Number-patterns.pdf-0009-22.png](images/Textbook_Mathematics_Gr10_Number-patterns.pdf-0009-22.png)


---

### Perfect Squares and Cubes

*   **Perfect Square:** A number obtained when an integer is squared.
    *   Example: $3^2 = 9$, therefore $9$ is a perfect square.
*   **Perfect Cube:** A number obtained when an integer is cubed.
    *   Example: $3^3 = 27$, therefore $27$ is a perfect cube.

---

### Estimating Surds

Surds that are not perfect roots can be estimated by finding the two consecutive integers (integers that follow one another on the number line) between which the surd lies.

**Example:** Consider $\sqrt[3]{52}$.
*   Since $3^3 = 27$ and $4^3 = 64$, and $27 < 52 < 64$, the value of $\sqrt[3]{52}$ lies between $3$ and $4$.



![Textbook_Mathematics_Gr10_Number-patterns.pdf-0010-07.png](images/Textbook_Mathematics_Gr10_Number-patterns.pdf-0010-07.png)


---

### Worked Example 5: Estimating Surds

**Question:** Find two consecutive integers such that $\sqrt{26}$ lies between them.

**Solution:**
*   **Step 1:** Use perfect squares to estimate the lower integer.
    $5^2 = 25$. Therefore, $5 < \sqrt{26}$.
*   **Step 2:** Use perfect squares to estimate the upper integer.
    $6^2 = 36$. Therefore, $\sqrt{26} < 6$.
*   **Step 3:** Write the final answer.
    $5 < \sqrt{26} < 6$

---

### Worked Example 6: Estimating Surds

**Question:** Find two consecutive integers such that $\sqrt[3]{49}$ lies between them.

**Solution:**
*   **Step 1:** Use perfect cubes to estimate the lower integer.
    $3^3 = 27$, therefore $3 < \sqrt[3]{49}$.
*   **Step 2:** Use perfect cubes to estimate the upper integer.
    $4^3 = 64$, therefore $\sqrt[3]{49} < 4$.
*   **Step 3:** Write the final answer.
    $3 < \sqrt[3]{49} < 4$
*   **Step 4:** Check the answer by cubing all terms in the inequality and simplifying.
    $27 < 49 < 64$. This is true, so $\sqrt[3]{49}$ lies between $3$ and $4$.

### 1.6 Products

Mathematical expressions are composed of specific parts. Understanding this terminology is essential for algebraic manipulation.

#### Parts of an Expression
Using the expression $3x^2 + 7xy - 5^3$ as an example:

| Name | Examples |
| :--- | :--- |
| term | $3x^2$ ; $7xy$ ; $-5^3$ |
| expression | $3x^2 + 7xy - 5^3$ |
| coefficient | $3$ ; $7$ |
| exponent | $2$ ; $1$ ; $3$ |
| base | $x$ ; $y$ ; $5$ |
| constant | $3$ ; $5$ |
| variable | $x$ ; $y$ |
| equation | $3x^2 + 7xy - 5^3 = 0$ |

---

### Multiplying a Monomial and a Binomial

*   **Monomial:** An expression with one term (e.g., $3x$ or $y^2$).
*   **Binomial:** An expression with two terms (e.g., $ax + b$ or $cx + d$).

To multiply a monomial by a binomial, use the distributive property: $a(b + c) = ab + ac$.

#### Worked Example 7: Simplifying brackets

**Question:**
Simplify: $2a(a - 1) - 3(a^2 - 1)$

**Solution:**
1. Distribute the $2a$ into the first bracket:
   $2a \times a = 2a^2$
   $2a \times (-1) = -2a$
   Result: $2a^2 - 2a$

2. Distribute the $-3$ into the second bracket:
   $-3 \times a^2 = -3a^2$
   $-3 \times (-1) = +3$
   Result: $-3a^2 + 3$

3. Combine the results:
   $2a^2 - 2a - 3a^2 + 3$

4. Group and add like terms:
   $(2a^2 - 3a^2) - 2a + 3$
   $= -a^2 - 2a + 3$

### Algebraic Expressions: Expansion

#### Worked Example: Distributive Property
**Question:** Simplify the expression $2a(a - 1) - 3(a^2 - 1)$

**Solution:**
$$2a(a - 1) - 3(a^2 - 1) = 2a(a) + 2a(-1) + (-3)(a^2) + (-3)(-1)$$
$$= 2a^2 - 2a - 3a^2 + 3$$
$$= -a^2 - 2a + 3$$

---

#### Multiplying Two Binomials
To multiply (or expand) two linear binomials, we use the distributive property:



![Textbook_Mathematics_Gr10_Number-patterns.pdf-0012-01.png](images/Textbook_Mathematics_Gr10_Number-patterns.pdf-0012-01.png)


$$(ax + b)(cx + d) = (ax)(cx) + (ax)(d) + b(cx) + bd$$
$$= acx^2 + adx + bcx + bd$$
$$= acx^2 + x(ad + bc) + bd$$

**Worked Example 8: Multiplying two binomials**
**Question:** Find the product: $(3x - 2)(5x + 8)$

**Solution:**
$$(3x - 2)(5x + 8) = (3x)(5x) + (3x)(8) + (-2)(5x) + (-2)(8)$$
$$= 15x^2 + 24x - 10x - 16$$
$$= 15x^2 + 14x - 16$$

---

#### Special Products
1. **Square of a binomial:** The product of two identical binomials.
   $$(ax + b)^2 = a^2x^2 + 2abx + b^2$$

2. **Difference of two squares:** The product of binomials with the same terms but opposite signs.
   $$(ax + b)(ax - b) = a^2x^2 - b^2$$

---

#### Multiplying a Binomial and a Trinomial
A trinomial is an expression with three terms, for example, $ax^2 + bx + c$. To multiply a binomial by a trinomial, distribute each term in the binomial to every term in the trinomial.

### Multiplying a Binomial and a Trinomial

To find the product of a binomial and a trinomial, you must multiply each term in the binomial by each term in the trinomial:

$$(A + B)(C + D + E) = A(C + D + E) + B(C + D + E)$$

---

### Worked Example 9: Multiplying a binomial and a trinomial

**Question:**
Find the product: $(x - 1)(x^2 - 2x + 1)$

**Solution:**

**Step 1: Expand the bracket**
$$(x - 1)(x^2 - 2x + 1) = x(x^2 - 2x + 1) - 1(x^2 - 2x + 1)$$
$$= x^3 - 2x^2 + x - x^2 + 2x - 1$$

**Step 2: Simplify**
$$(x - 1)(x^2 - 2x + 1) = x^3 - 3x^2 + 3x - 1$$

### 1.7 Factorisation

Factorisation is the reverse process of expanding brackets. While expansion involves multiplying terms to remove brackets, factorisation involves rewriting an expression as a product of its factors.

*   **Example:** 
    *   Expanding: $2(x + 1) = 2x + 2$
    *   Factorising: $2x + 2 = 2(x + 1)$



![Textbook_Mathematics_Gr10_Number-patterns.pdf-0015-10.png](images/Textbook_Mathematics_Gr10_Number-patterns.pdf-0015-10.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_Mathematics_Gr10_Number-patterns.pdf-0015-10.png]**
> A diagram of a number of dots and lines, with the number of dots and lines labeled.



The expressions $2(x + 1)$ and $2x + 2$ are equivalent, meaning they yield the same value for any value of $x$. In previous grades, factorisation techniques included taking out a common factor and identifying the difference of squares.

# Common Factors

Factorising based on common factors relies on identifying factors that are present in all terms of an algebraic expression.

### Basic Examples
1. To factorise $2x - 6x^2$:
   $$2x - 6x^2 = 2x(1 - 3x)$$

2. To factorise $2(x - 1) - a(x - 1)$:
   $$(x - 1)(2 - a)$$

---

### Worked Example: Factorising using a "switch around"
**Question:** Factorise $5(a - 2) - b(2 - a)$

**Solution:**
Use the "switch around" strategy to identify a common factor. Note that $2 - a = -(a - 2)$.

$$5(a - 2) - b(2 - a) = 5(a - 2) - [-b(a - 2)]$$
$$= 5(a - 2) + b(a - 2)$$
$$= (a - 2)(5 + b)$$

# Difference of Two Squares

## Concept Definition
The expansion of the product $(ax + b)(ax - b)$ results in $a^2x^2 - b^2$. Conversely, this means that an expression in the form $a^2x^2 - b^2$ can be factorised into $(ax + b)(ax - b)$.

**Example:**
$x^2 - 16$ can be written as $x^2 - 4^2$. Therefore, the factors are $(x - 4)(x + 4)$.

## How to Identify a Difference of Two Squares
To identify if an expression is a difference of two squares, look for these three criteria:
1. The expression consists of exactly **two terms**.
2. The terms have **different signs** (one positive, one negative).
3. Each term is a **perfect square**.

**Examples:**
* $a^2 - 1$
* $4x^2 - y^2$
* $-49 + p^4$ (which is $p^4 - 49$)

---

## Worked Example 11: The difference of two squares

**Question:**
Factorise: $3a(a^2 - 4) - 7(a^2 - 4)$

**Solution:**

**Step 1: Take out the common factor $(a^2 - 4)$**
$$3a(a^2 - 4) - 7(a^2 - 4) = (a^2 - 4)(3a - 7)$$

**Step 2: Factorise the difference of two squares $(a^2 - 4)$**
$$(a^2 - 4)(3a - 7) = (a - 2)(a + 2)(3a - 7)$$

---

### Factorising by Grouping in Pairs

Factorising by grouping is a technique used when an expression has four terms that do not share a single common factor across all of them, but can be grouped into pairs that share common factors.

#### The Concept
1. **Identify groups:** Split the four terms into two pairs.
2. **Factorise each pair:** Take out the common factor from each pair individually.
3. **Identify the common binomial:** If done correctly, both groups will share a common binomial factor.
4. **Final factorisation:** Take out the common binomial factor to reach the final result.

**Example:**
Factorise $2x^2 + 2x + 3x + 3$

*   **Step 1:** Group the terms: $(2x^2 + 2x) + (3x + 3)$
*   **Step 2:** Factorise each group: $2x(x + 1) + 3(x + 1)$
*   **Step 3:** Factor out the common binomial $(x + 1)$: $(x + 1)(2x + 3)$

---

#### Worked Example 12: Factorising by grouping in pairs

**Question:** Find the factors of $7x + 14y + bx + 2by$.

**Solution:**
*   **Step 1:** Observe that there is no factor common to all four terms.
*   **Step 2:** Group terms that share common factors:
    *   Group 1: $(7x + 14y)$ which has a common factor of $7$.
    *   Group 2: $(bx + 2by)$ which has a common factor of $b$.
*   **Step 3:** Apply the grouping:
    $$7x + 14y + bx + 2by = (7x + 14y) + (bx + 2by)$$
    $$= 7(x + 2y) + b(x + 2y)$$
    $$= (x + 2y)(7 + b)$$

---

### Factorisation by Grouping

Factorisation by grouping is a method used when an expression has four or more terms that do not share a single common factor across all terms. Instead, we group terms that share common factors.

#### Worked Example: Factorise $7x + 14y + bx + 2by$

**Method 1: Direct Common Factor**
If a common binomial factor is already visible:
$$7(x + 2y) + b(x + 2y) = (x + 2y)(7 + b)$$

**Method 2: Grouping Terms**
If the common factor is not immediately obvious, follow these steps:

1.  **Group terms with common factors:** Identify pairs that share a common factor.
    *   $x$ is a common factor of the first and third terms ($7x$ and $bx$).
    *   $2y$ is a common factor of the second and fourth terms ($14y$ and $2by$).
2.  **Rearrange and factorise:**
    $$7x + 14y + bx + 2by = (7x + bx) + (14y + 2by)$$
    $$= x(7 + b) + 2y(7 + b)$$
3.  **Take out the common binomial factor:**
    $$x(7 + b) + 2y(7 + b) = (7 + b)(x + 2y)$$

**Final Answer:** The factors are $(7 + b)$ and $(x + 2y)$.

# Factorising a Quadratic Trinomial

## Introduction to Factorisation
Factorisation is the reverse process of calculating the product of factors. To factorise a quadratic expression, we must find the factors which, when multiplied together, equal the original quadratic expression.

---

## Basic Factorisation Methods

### 1. Common Factors
For expressions of the form $ax^2 + bx$, we identify the common factor $x$.
*   **Example:** $ax^2 + bx = x(ax + b)$
*   **Example:** $8y^2 + 4y = 4y(2y + 1)$

### 2. Difference of Squares
We use the identity for the difference of squares:
$$(a + b)(a - b) = a^2 - b^2$$
Therefore, any expression in the form $a^2 - b^2$ can be written in the factorised form $(a + b)(a - b)$.

---

## Factorising Trinomials ($x^2 + bx + c$)

To factorise more complex quadratics, we examine the expansion of two binomials:
$$(x + 2)(x + 3) = x^2 + 3x + 2x + 6 = x^2 + 5x + 6$$

**Key Observations:**
*   The $x^2$ term is the product of the $x$-terms in each bracket.
*   The constant term (e.g., $6$) is the product of the two constants in the brackets.
*   The middle term (e.g., $5x$) is the sum of the two terms.

### Step-by-Step Strategy
To factorise $x^2 + 5x + 6$:

1.  **Set up the brackets:**
    $(x \quad )(x \quad )$
2.  **Identify factors of the constant term ($6$):**
    Possible pairs: $(1, 6)$, $(2, 3)$, $(-1, -6)$, $(-2, -3)$.
3.  **Test the combinations:**

| Option | Factors |
| :--- | :--- |
| Option 1 | $(x + 1)(x + 6)$ |
| Option 2 | $(x - 1)(x - 6)$ |
| Option 3 | $(x + 2)(x + 3)$ |
| Option 4 | $(x - 2)(x - 3)$ |

4.  **Expand to verify the middle term:**

| Option | Expansion | Result |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Option 1 | $(x + 1)(x + 6)$ | $x^2 + 7x + 6$ |
| Option 2 | $(x - 1)(x - 6)$ | $x^2 - 7x + 6$ |
| Option 3 | $(x + 2)(x + 3)$ | $x^2 + 5x + 6$ |
| Option 4 | $(x - 2)(x - 3)$ | $x^2 - 5x + 6$ |

**Conclusion:** Option 3, $(x + 2)(x + 3)$, is the correct factorisation for $x^2 + 5x + 6$.

### General Procedure for Factorising a Trinomial

To factorise a quadratic trinomial of the form $ax^2 + bx + c$, follow these steps:

1.  **Common Factor:** Take out any common factor in the coefficients to obtain an expression where $a, b,$ and $c$ have no common factors and $a$ is positive.
2.  **Setup Brackets:** Write down two brackets with an $x$ in each bracket and space for the remaining terms: $(x \quad )(x \quad )$.
3.  **Identify Factors:** List the factors for $a$ and $c$.
4.  **Test Combinations:** Write down a set of options for the possible factors of the quadratic using the factors of $a$ and $c$.
5.  **Expand:** Expand all options to determine which one results in the correct middle term $bx$.

#### Important Rules for Signs
*   If $c$ is positive: The factors of $c$ must be either both positive or both negative.
*   If $c$ is negative: One factor of $c$ must be negative, and the other must be positive.
*   **Verification:** Always multiply your brackets again after finding an answer to ensure the expansion matches the original expression.

---

### Worked Example 13: Factorising a Quadratic Trinomial

**Question:** Factorise $3x^2 + 2x - 1$.

**Solution:**

**Step 1:** Ensure the quadratic is in the form $ax^2 + bx + c$.
**Step 2:** Identify factors for $a$ and $c$.
*   Possible factors for $a$ ($3$): $1$ and $3$.
*   Possible factors for $c$ ($-1$): $-1$ and $1$.

**Step 3:** Test combinations to find the correct middle term.

| Option 1 | Option 2 |
| :--- | :--- |
| $(x - 1)(3x + 1)$ | $(x + 1)(3x - 1)$ |
| $3x^2 - 2x - 1$ | $3x^2 + 2x - 1$ |

**Step 4:** Verify the solution by expanding the correct option.
$$(x + 1)(3x - 1) = 3x^2 - x + 3x - 1$$
$$= 3x^2 + 2x - 1$$

**Final Answer:**
$$3x^2 + 2x - 1 = (x + 1)(3x - 1)$$

### Sum and Difference of Two Cubes

Special algebraic identities are used to factorise expressions involving the sum or difference of cubes.

#### 1. Sum of Two Cubes
By multiplying a binomial and a trinomial, we derive the identity:
$$(x + y)(x^2 - xy + y^2) = x(x^2 - xy + y^2) + y(x^2 - xy + y^2)$$
$$= [x(x^2) + x(-xy) + x(y^2)] + [y(x^2) + y(-xy) + y(y^2)]$$
$$= x^3 - x^2y + xy^2 + x^2y - xy^2 + y^3$$
$$= x^3 + y^3$$

**Identity:**
$$x^3 + y^3 = (x + y)(x^2 - xy + y^2)$$

#### 2. Difference of Two Cubes
Similarly, for the difference of two cubes:
$$(x - y)(x^2 + xy + y^2) = x(x^2 + xy + y^2) - y(x^2 + xy + y^2)$$
$$= [x(x^2) + x(xy) + x(y^2)] - [y(x^2) + y(xy) + y(y^2)]$$
$$= x^3 + x^2y + xy^2 - x^2y - xy^2 - y^3$$
$$= x^3 - y^3$$

**Identity:**
$$x^3 - y^3 = (x - y)(x^2 + xy + y^2)$$

### Factorisation of Cubes

#### 1. Difference of Two Cubes
The general formula for the difference of two cubes is:
$$x^3 - y^3 = (x - y)(x^2 + xy + y^2)$$

**Worked Example 14: Factorise $a^3 - 1$**

*   **Step 1: Take the cube root of terms that are perfect cubes.**
    Identify $x$ and $y$ by taking the cube roots:
    $\sqrt[3]{a^3} = a$ and $\sqrt[3]{1} = 1$.
    Therefore, $x = a$ and $y = 1$.

*   **Step 2: Find the three terms in the second bracket.**
    Substitute $a$ and $1$ into the formula:
    $$(a^3 - 1) = (a - 1)(a^2 + a + 1)$$

*   **Step 3: Expand the brackets to check the result.**
    $$(a - 1)(a^2 + a + 1) = a(a^2 + a + 1) - 1(a^2 + a + 1)$$
    $$= a^3 + a^2 + a - a^2 - a - 1$$
    $$= a^3 - 1$$

---

#### 2. Sum of Two Cubes
The general formula for the sum of two cubes is:
$$x^3 + y^3 = (x + y)(x^2 - xy + y^2)$$

**Worked Example 15: Factorise $x^3 + 8$**

*   **Step 1: Take the cube root of terms that are perfect cubes.**
    Identify $x$ and $y$ by taking the cube roots:
    $\sqrt[3]{x^3} = x$ and $\sqrt[3]{8} = 2$.
    Therefore, $x = x$ and $y = 2$.

*   **Step 2: Find the three terms in the second bracket.**
    Substitute $x$ and $2$ into the formula:
    $$(x^3 + 8) = (x + 2)(x^2 - 2x + 4)$$

*   **Step 3: Expand the brackets to check the result.**
    $$(x + 2)(x^2 - 2x + 4) = x(x^2 - 2x + 4) + 2(x^2 - 2x + 4)$$
    $$= x^3 - 2x^2 + 4x + 2x^2 - 4x + 8$$
    $$= x^3 + 8$$

### Factorising Sums and Differences of Cubes

To factorise expressions involving the sum or difference of two cubes, we use the following standard algebraic identities:

1.  **Difference of two cubes:** $x^3 - y^3 = (x - y)(x^2 + xy + y^2)$
2.  **Sum of two cubes:** $x^3 + y^3 = (x + y)(x^2 - xy + y^2)$

---

### Worked Example 16: Factorising a difference of two cubes

**Question:** Factorise $16y^3 - 432$.

**Solution:**

**Step 1: Take out the common factor**
First, identify the highest common factor (HCF) for both terms:
$$16y^3 - 432 = 16(y^3 - 27)$$

**Step 2: Identify the cube roots**
We are working with the difference of two cubes. We identify $x$ and $y$ by taking the cube roots of the terms inside the bracket:
*   $\sqrt[3]{y^3} = y$
*   $\sqrt[3]{27} = 3$
So, $x = y$ and $y = 3$.

**Step 3: Apply the identity**
Substitute $x = y$ and $y = 3$ into the formula $x^3 - y^3 = (x - y)(x^2 + xy + y^2)$:
$$16(y^3 - 27) = 16(y - 3)(y^2 + 3y + 9)$$

**Step 4: Verification (Expansion)**
To ensure the factorisation is correct, expand the brackets:
| Step | Calculation |
| :--- | :--- |
| Distribute | $16[y(y^2 + 3y + 9) - 3(y^2 + 3y + 9)]$ |
| Expand | $16[y^3 + 3y^2 + 9y - 3y^2 - 9y - 27]$ |
| Simplify | $16[y^3 - 27]$ |
| Final Result | $16y^3 - 432$ |

---

### Worked Example 17: Factorising a sum of two cubes

**Question:** Factorise $8t^3 + 125p^3$.

**Solution:**

**Step 1: Take the cube root of terms that are perfect cubes**
We are working with the sum of two cubes. We use the identity $x^3 + y^3 = (x + y)(x^2 - xy + y^2)$.

Identify $x$ and $y$ by taking the cube roots:
*   $\sqrt[3]{8t^3} = 2t$
*   $\sqrt[3]{125p^3} = 5p$

Therefore, $x = 2t$ and $y = 5p$.

**Step 2: Apply the identity**
Substitute $x = 2t$ and $y = 5p$ into the formula:
$$(2t + 5p)((2t)^2 - (2t)(5p) + (5p)^2)$$
$$(2t + 5p)(4t^2 - 10tp + 25p^2)$$

### Factorisation: Sum and Difference of Cubes

#### Worked Example: Factoring $8t^3 + 125p^3$

**Step 2: Find the three terms in the second bracket**
We can replace $x$ and $y$ in the factorised form of the expression for the sum of two cubes with $2t$ and $5p$. Doing so, we get the second bracket:

$$(8t^3 + 125p^3) = (2t + 5p) [(2t)^2 - (2t)(5p) + (5p)^2]$$
$$= (2t + 5p) (4t^2 - 10tp + 25p^2)$$

**Step 3: Expand the brackets to check the factorisation**
To verify the result, expand the expression:

$$(2t + 5p) (4t^2 - 10tp + 25p^2) = 2t(4t^2 - 10tp + 25p^2) + 5p(4t^2 - 10tp + 25p^2)$$
$$= 8t^3 - 20tp^2 + 50tp^2 + 20tp^2 - 50p^2t + 125p^3$$
$$= 8t^3 + 125p^3$$

# 1.8 Simplification of Fractions

## Fundamental Rules for Fractions
When working with algebraic fractions, the following rules apply (where denominators are non-zero):

1. **Multiplication:** $\frac{a}{b} \times \frac{c}{d} = \frac{ac}{bd} \quad (b \neq 0; d \neq 0)$
2. **Addition:** $\frac{a}{b} + \frac{c}{b} = \frac{a + c}{b} \quad (b \neq 0)$
3. **Division:** $\frac{a}{b} \div \frac{c}{d} = \frac{a}{b} \times \frac{d}{c} = \frac{ad}{bc} \quad (b \neq 0; c \neq 0; d \neq 0)$

**Note:** Dividing by a fraction is equivalent to multiplying by the reciprocal of that fraction.

---

## Simplifying Algebraic Fractions
To simplify algebraic expressions that are fractions, you must factorise the numerator and the denominator completely before cancelling common terms.

**Example:**
Simplify $\frac{x^2 + 3x}{x + 3}$

1. Factorise the numerator: $x^2 + 3x = x(x + 3)$
2. Simplify the expression:
   $$\frac{x(x + 3)}{x + 3} = x \quad (x \neq -3)$$

**Important:** Always state the restrictions on the variable. If $x = -3$, the denominator becomes $0$, making the fraction undefined.

---

## Worked Example 18: Simplifying Fractions

**Question:**
Simplify the following expression:
$$\frac{ax - b + x - ab}{ax^2 - abx}, \quad (x \neq 0; x \neq b)$$

**Solution:**

**Step 1: Use grouping to factorise the numerator and take out the common factor $ax$ in the denominator.**
$$\frac{(ax - ab) + (x - b)}{ax^2 - abx} = \frac{a(x - b) + (x - b)}{ax(x - b)}$$

**Step 2: Take out the common factor $(x - b)$ in the numerator.**
$$= \frac{(x - b)(a + 1)}{ax(x - b)}$$

**Step 3: Cancel the common factor $(x - b)$ to reach the final simplified form.**
$$= \frac{a + 1}{ax}$$

### Simplifying Algebraic Fractions

#### Worked Example 19: Simplifying Fractions
**Question:**
Simplify the following expression:
$$\frac{x^2 - x - 2}{x^2 - 4} \div \frac{x^2 + x}{x^2 + 2x}, \quad (x \neq 0; x \neq \pm 2)$$

**Solution:**

**Step 1: Factorise the numerator and denominator**
Factorise each quadratic expression:
$$= \frac{(x + 1)(x - 2)}{(x + 2)(x - 2)} \div \frac{x(x + 1)}{x(x + 2)}$$

**Step 2: Change the division sign and multiply by the reciprocal**
To divide by a fraction, multiply by its reciprocal:
$$= \frac{(x + 1)(x - 2)}{(x + 2)(x - 2)} \times \frac{x(x + 2)}{x(x + 1)}$$

**Step 3: Write the final answer**
Cancel the common factors in the numerator and denominator:
$$= 1$$

---

#### Worked Example 20: Simplifying Fractions
**Question:**
Simplify the following expression:
$$\frac{x - 2}{x^2 - 4} + \frac{x^2}{x - 2} - \frac{x^3 + x - 4}{x^2 - 4}, \quad (x \neq \pm 2)$$

**Solution:**

**Step 1: Factorise the denominators**
Identify the common denominator, which is $(x + 2)(x - 2)$:
$$\frac{x - 2}{(x + 2)(x - 2)} + \frac{x^2}{x - 2} - \frac{x^3 + x - 4}{(x + 2)(x - 2)}$$

*(Note: To continue this simplification, one would find a common denominator for all terms, expand the numerators, and combine like terms.)*

### Simplifying Algebraic Fractions

#### Worked Example: Continued Steps
Following the process of simplifying algebraic fractions by finding a common denominator:

**Step 2: Make all denominators the same**
The lowest common denominator is $(x - 2)(x + 2)$.
$$\frac{x - 2}{(x + 2)(x - 2)} + \frac{(x^2)(x + 2)}{(x + 2)(x - 2)} - \frac{x^3 + x - 4}{(x + 2)(x - 2)}$$

**Step 3: Write as one fraction**
$$\frac{x - 2 + (x^2)(x + 2) - (x^3 + x - 4)}{(x + 2)(x - 2)}$$

**Step 4: Simplify**
$$\frac{x - 2 + x^3 + 2x^2 - x^3 - x + 4}{(x + 2)(x - 2)} = \frac{2x^2 + 2}{(x + 2)(x - 2)}$$

**Step 5: Take out the common factor and write the final answer**
$$\frac{2(x^2 + 1)}{(x + 2)(x - 2)}$$

---

### Worked Example 21: Simplifying fractions

**QUESTION**
Simplify:
$$\frac{2}{x^2 - x} + \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^3 - 1} - \frac{x}{x^2 - 1}, \quad (x \neq 0; x \neq \pm 1)$$

**SOLUTION**

**Step 1: Factorise the numerator and denominator**
$$\frac{2}{x(x - 1)} + \frac{(x^2 + x + 1)}{(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)} - \frac{x}{(x - 1)(x + 1)}$$

**Step 2: Simplify and find the common denominator**
Note: The middle term simplifies to $\frac{1}{x-1}$. The common denominator is $x(x - 1)(x + 1)$.
$$\frac{2(x + 1) + x(x + 1) - x^2}{x(x - 1)(x + 1)}$$

**Step 3: Write the final answer**
$$\frac{2x + 2 + x^2 + x - x^2}{x(x - 1)(x + 1)} = \frac{3x + 2}{x(x - 1)(x + 1)}$$

i) $\frac{k + 2}{k^2 + 2} - \frac{1}{k + 2}$

j) $\frac{t + 2}{3q} + \frac{t + 1}{2q}$

k) $\frac{3}{p^2 - 4} + \frac{2}{(p - 2)^2}$

l) $\frac{x}{x + y} + \frac{x^2}{y^2 - x^2}$

m) $\frac{1}{m + n} + \frac{3mn}{m^3 + n^3}$

n) $\frac{h}{h^3 - f^3} - \frac{1}{h^2 + hf + f^2}$

#### 3. Chapter Summary: Number Systems
*   **Natural numbers ($\mathbb{N}$):** $\{1; 2; 3; \dots\}$
*   **Whole numbers ($\mathbb{N}_0$):** $\{0; 1; 2; 3; \dots\}$
*   **Integers ($\mathbb{Z}$):** $\{\dots; -3; -2; -1; 0; 1; 2; 3; \dots\}$
*   **Rational Numbers:** Any number that can be written as $\frac{a}{b}$ where $a$ and $b$ are integers and $b \neq 0$.

**Types of Rational Numbers:**
*   Fractions with both numerator and denominator as integers.
*   Integers.
*   Decimal numbers that terminate.
*   Decimal numbers that recur (repeat).

### 1. Number Systems and Surds

*   **Irrational Numbers:** Numbers that cannot be written as a fraction $\frac{p}{q}$ where $p$ and $q$ are integers.
*   **Surds:** If the $n^{th}$ root of a number cannot be simplified to a rational number, it is called a surd.
*   **Comparison Rule:** If $a$ and $b$ are positive whole numbers, and $a < b$, then $\sqrt[n]{a} < \sqrt[n]{b}$.

---

### 2. Algebraic Expressions and Factorisation

*   **Binomial:** An expression with two terms.
*   **Square of a Binomial:** The product of two identical binomials.
*   **Difference of Two Squares:** The result of multiplying $(ax + b)(ax - b)$.
*   **Factorisation:** The process of breaking an expression into its factors (the opposite of expanding brackets).
*   **Expansion of a Binomial and Trinomial:**
    $$(A + B)(C + D + E) = A(C + D + E) + B(C + D + E)$$
*   **Factorisation Methods:**
    *   Taking out a common factor.
    *   Grouping.
    *   Quadratic factorisation (finding the two binomials that produce the quadratic).
*   **Sum of Two Cubes:**
    $$x^3 + y^3 = (x + y)(x^2 - xy + y^2)$$
*   **Difference of Two Cubes:**
    $$x^3 - y^3 = (x - y)(x^2 + xy + y^2)$$

---

### 3. Algebraic Fractions

*   **Simplification:** Use factorisation methods to simplify fractions.
*   **Cancellation Rule:** Only factors can be cancelled out in fractions, never individual terms.
*   **Addition/Subtraction:** To add or subtract fractions, the denominators must be the same.

### 4. Properties of Integers and Irrational Numbers
If $a$ is an integer, $b$ is an integer, and $c$ is irrational, which of the following numbers are rational?
a) $\frac{-b}{a}$
b) $c \div c$
c) $\frac{a}{c}$
d) $\frac{1}{c}$

### 5. Classification of Numbers

$\sqrt[3]{26} ; \frac{3}{2} ; \sqrt{-24} ; \sqrt{39} ; 7,11 ; \pi^2 ; \frac{\pi}{2} ; 7,12 ; -\sqrt{24} ; \frac{\sqrt{2}}{0} ; 3\pi ; \sqrt{78} ; 9 ; \pi$

a) Which of the numbers are non-real numbers?
b) Without using a calculator, rank all the real numbers in ascending order.
c) Which of the numbers are irrational numbers?
d) Which of the numbers are rational numbers?
e) Which of the numbers are integers?
f) Which of the numbers are undefined?

### Algebraic Expressions: Exercises

#### Simplify the following expressions:

e) $\frac{m^2 + 11m + 18}{4(m^2 - 4)} \div \frac{3m^2 + 27m}{24m^2 - 48m}$

f) $\frac{t^2 + 9t + 18}{5(t^2 - 9)} \div \frac{4t^2 + 24t}{100t^2 - 300t}$

g) $\frac{4 - b^2}{3b - 6}$

h) $\frac{x^2 + 2x + 4}{x^3 - 8}$

i) $\frac{x^2 - 5x - 14}{3x + 6}$

j) $\frac{d^2 + 23d + 132}{5(d^2 - 121)} \div \frac{4d^2 + 48d}{100d^2 - 1100d}$

k) $\frac{a - 2}{a^2 + 4a + 3} \div \frac{(a - 1)(a + 1)}{a - 1} \times \frac{a^2 - 2a - 15}{a - 2}$

l) $\frac{a + 6}{a^2 + 12a + 11} \times \frac{a^2 + 14a + 33}{a + 3} \div \frac{a^3 + 216}{a + 1}$

m) $2 \div \frac{a + b}{a + 2b} \times \frac{b^2 - ba - 6a^2}{a^2 - 4b^2} \times \frac{a^2 - b - 2b^2}{3a - b}$

n) $\frac{st + sb + 31t + 31b}{t + b}$

o) $\frac{ny + nq + 8y + 8q}{y + q}$

p) $\frac{p^2 - q^2}{p} \div \frac{p + q}{p^2 - pq}$

q) $\frac{2}{x} + \frac{x}{2} - \frac{2x}{3}$

r) $\frac{1}{a + 7} - \frac{a + 7}{a^2 - 49}$

s) $\frac{x + 2}{2x^3} + 16$

t) $\frac{1 - 2a}{4a^2 - 1} - \frac{a - 1}{2a^2 - 3a + 1} - \frac{1}{1 - a}$

u) $\frac{1}{2}x + \frac{x - 2}{3} + 4$

v) $\frac{1}{x^2 + 2x} + \frac{4x^2 - x - 3}{x^2 + 2x - 3}$

w) $\frac{b^2 + 6b + 9}{b^2 - 9} + \frac{b^2 - 6b + 8}{(b - 2)(b + 3)} + \frac{1}{b + 3}$

x) $\frac{x^2 + 2x}{x^2 + x + 6} \times \frac{x^2 + 2x + 1}{x^2 + 3x + 2}$

y) $\frac{12}{z + 12} + \frac{5}{z - 5}$

z) $\frac{11}{w - 11} - \frac{4}{w - 4}$

#### Algebraic Problems

32. Show that $(2x - 1)^2 - (x - 3)^2$ can be simplified to $(x + 2)(3x - 4)$.

33. What must be added to $x^2 - x + 4$ to make it equal to $(x + 2)^2$?

34. Evaluate $\frac{x^3 + 1}{x^2 - x + 1}$ if $x = 7,85$ without using a calculator. Show your work.

35. With what expression must $(a - 2b)$ be multiplied to get a product of $(a^3 - 8b^3)$?

36. With what expression must $27x^3 + 1$ be divided to get a quotient of $3x + 1$?

37. What are the restrictions on the following?

a) $\frac{4}{3x^2 + 2x - 1}$

b) $\frac{a}{3(b - a) + ab - a^2}$

# 1. Algebraic Expressions

## 1.1 Introduction
This chapter covers foundational algebraic skills essential for further mathematical study. Key focus areas include:
*   Classification of numbers (rational vs. irrational).
*   Estimation of surds.
*   Rounding of real numbers.
*   Simplification and factorisation (including binomials, trinomials, grouping in pairs, and sum/difference of two cubes).

## 1.2 The Real Number System
The real number system encompasses various sets of numbers. Understanding the hierarchy of these sets is crucial for classification.

## 1.3 Rational and Irrational Numbers
*   **Decimal numbers:** Can be terminating or recurring.
*   **Converting terminating decimals:** Expressing decimals as fractions in the form $\frac{a}{b}$.
*   **Converting recurring decimals:** Using algebraic methods to express repeating decimals as rational fractions.

---

ii. Every natural number is a whole number. (**True**)

### Number Systems and Sets

#### 1. Understanding Venn Diagrams of Number Sets
The relationship between Natural numbers ($\mathbb{N}$), Whole numbers ($\mathbb{N}_0$), and Integers ($\mathbb{Z}$) can be represented using a Venn diagram.



![Textbook_Mathematics_Gr10_Number-patterns.pdf-0037-00.png](images/Textbook_Mathematics_Gr10_Number-patterns.pdf-0037-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_Mathematics_Gr10_Number-patterns.pdf-0037-00.png]**
> A diagram of a wave with a blue background and a brown border. The wave is composed of multiple lines and arrows, with the arrows pointing in different directions. The diagram is labeled with numbers and arrows, and the labels include "1", "2", "3", "4", "5", "6", "7", "8", "9", and "10". The diagram also



*   **Natural numbers ($\mathbb{N}$):** $\{1; 2; 3; \dots\}$
*   **Whole numbers ($\mathbb{N}_0$):** $\{0; 1; 2; 3; \dots\}$
*   **Integers ($\mathbb{Z}$):** $\{\dots; -3; -2; -1; 0; 1; 2; 3; \dots\}$

**Key Concept:**
*   $\mathbb{N}$ is a subset of $\mathbb{N}_0$.
*   $\mathbb{N}_0$ is a subset of $\mathbb{Z}$.
*   Numbers that are not integers (e.g., fractions like $-\frac{1}{2}$) fall outside the circles representing $\mathbb{N}, \mathbb{N}_0,$ and $\mathbb{Z}$ but remain within the universal set (the rectangle).

---

#### 2. Evaluating Statements about Number Sets
When determining if a statement about number sets is true or false, consider the definitions:

*   **Example:** Which of the following is true?
    *   i. Every integer is a natural number. (False: Negative integers are not natural numbers).
    *   ii. Every whole number is an integer. (True: The set of whole numbers is contained within the set of integers).
    *   iii. There are no decimals in the whole numbers. (True: Whole numbers increase in steps of 1).

---

#### 3. Real, Non-Real, and Undefined Numbers
To classify numbers, apply the following rules:
*   **Real:** A number is real if it can be represented on the number line. Square roots of negative numbers are not real.
*   **Non-real:** A number is non-real if it involves the square root of a negative number (e.g., $\sqrt{-9}$).
*   **Undefined:** A number is undefined if it involves division by zero (e.g., $\frac{a}{0}$).

**Worked Examples:**

| Expression | Classification | Reason |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| $-\sqrt{3}$ | Real | No negative under the root; not divided by zero. |
| $\frac{0}{\sqrt{2}}$ | Real | Not divided by zero. |
| $\sqrt{-9}$ | Non-real | Negative sign under the square root. |
| $\frac{-\sqrt{7}}{0}$ | Undefined | Division by zero is not permitted. |
| $-\sqrt{-16}$ | Non-real | Negative sign under the square root. |

### 4. Classification of Numbers
State whether the following numbers are rational or irrational. If the number is rational, state whether it is a natural number, whole number, or an integer.

| Expression | Classification | Reasoning |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| a) $-\frac{1}{3}$ | Rational | A fraction of integers is a rational number. |
| b) $0,651268962154862...$ | Irrational | It cannot be simplified to a fraction of integers. |
| c) $\frac{\sqrt{9}}{3}$ | Rational, Integer, Whole, Natural | $\frac{\sqrt{9}}{3} = \frac{3}{3} = 1$. An integer is a rational number. |
| d) $\pi^2$ | Irrational | It cannot be simplified to a fraction of integers. |
| e) $\pi^4$ | Irrational | It cannot be simplified to a fraction of integers. |
| f) $\sqrt[3]{19}$ | Irrational | It cannot be simplified to a fraction of integers. |
| g) $(\sqrt[3]{1})^7$ | Rational, Integer, Whole, Natural | $(\sqrt[3]{1})^7 = 1^7 = 1$. It can be written as an integer. |
| h) $\pi + 3$ | Irrational | $\pi$ is irrational. Any rational number added to an irrational number is irrational. |
| i) $\pi + 0,858408346$ | Irrational | $\pi$ is irrational and $0,858408346$ is rational. The sum is irrational. |

---

### 5. Rationality with Variables
If $a$ is an integer, $b$ is an integer, and $c$ is irrational, determine which of the following are rational numbers:

**a) $\frac{5}{6}$**
*   **Solution:** $\frac{5}{6}$ is rational.

**b) $\frac{a}{3}$**
*   **Solution:** Since $a$ is an integer, $\frac{a}{3}$ is rational.

**c) $\frac{-2}{b}$**
*   **Solution:** Since $b$ is an integer, $\frac{-2}{b}$ is rational. Note that $b$ cannot be $0$ as that makes the fraction undefined.

**d) $\frac{1}{c}$**
*   **Solution:** (Incomplete in source text, but mathematically: Since $c$ is irrational, $\frac{1}{c}$ is irrational).

### 6. Determining Rationality of Expressions
For each value of $a$, determine whether $\frac{a}{14}$ is rational or irrational.

*   **a) $a = 1$**
    *   Solution: $\frac{1}{14}$ is **rational**.
*   **b) $a = -10$**
    *   Solution: $\frac{-10}{14}$ is **rational**.
*   **c) $a = \sqrt{2}$**
    *   Solution: $\frac{\sqrt{2}}{14}$ is **irrational**.
*   **d) $a = 2,1$**
    *   Solution: $\frac{2,1}{14}$ is **rational**.

### 7. Classifying Numbers
Consider the following list of numbers:
$-3 ; 0 ; \sqrt{-1} ; -8\frac{4}{5} ; -\sqrt{8} ; \frac{22}{7} ; \frac{14}{0} ; 7 ; 1,\overline{34} ; 3,3231089... ; 3 + \sqrt{2} ; 9\frac{7}{10} ; \pi ; 11$

| Category | Numbers |
| :--- | :--- |
| **a) Natural numbers** $\{1; 2; 3; ...\}$ | $7, 11$ |
| **b) Irrational numbers** | $-\sqrt{8}, 3,3231089..., 3 + \sqrt{2}, \pi$ |
| **c) Non-real numbers** | $\sqrt{-1}$ |
| **d) Rational numbers** | $-3, 0, -8\frac{4}{5}, \frac{22}{7}, 7, 1,\overline{34}, 9\frac{7}{10}, 11$ |
| **e) Integers** $\{\dots; -2; -1; 0; 1; 2; \dots\}$ | $-3, 7, 11$ |
| **f) Undefined** | $\frac{14}{0}$ |

### 8. Identifying Patterns and Rationality
For each number, write the next three digits and state whether the number is rational or irrational.

**a) $1,1\dot{5}$**
*   **Next three digits:** $555$ (The dot indicates the $5$ repeats).
*   **Classification:** **Rational**, because there is a repeating pattern of digits.

**b) $2,121314...$**
*   **Next three digits:** Cannot be determined (The sequence does not terminate and shows no clear repeating pattern).
*   **Classification:** **Irrational**, because there is no repeating pattern.

---

### Identifying Rational and Irrational Numbers

#### Analysis of Non-terminating Decimals
When a number does not terminate (indicated by $...$), we must determine if there is a repeating pattern.

*   **Example c) $1,242244246...$**
    *   **Observation:** The number does not terminate. There is no indication of a repeating pattern of digits (no dot or bar notation).
    *   **Conclusion:** Irrational, as there is no repeating pattern.

*   **Example d) $3,324354...$**
    *   **Observation:** The number does not terminate. There is no indication of a repeating pattern of digits.
    *   **Conclusion:** Irrational, as there is no repeating pattern.

*   **Example e) $3,3243\dot{5}\dot{4}$**
    *   **Observation:** The dot over the $5$ and $4$ indicates that the pattern $54$ repeats. The next three digits are $545$.
    *   **Conclusion:** Rational, as there is a repeating pattern.

---

### Converting Decimals to Fractions

To convert a terminating decimal to a fraction, express the decimal as a sum of place values and simplify.

#### a) $0,1$
$$0,1 = \frac{1}{10}$$

#### b) $0,12$
$$0,12 = \frac{1}{10} + \frac{2}{100}$$
$$= \frac{10}{100} + \frac{2}{100}$$
$$= \frac{12}{100}$$
$$= \frac{3}{25}$$

#### c) $0,58$
$$0,58 = \frac{5}{10} + \frac{8}{100}$$
$$= \frac{50}{100} + \frac{8}{100}$$
$$= \frac{58}{100}$$
$$= \frac{29}{50}$$

#### d) $0,2589$
$$0,2589 = \frac{2}{10} + \frac{5}{100} + \frac{8}{1000} + \frac{9}{10000}$$
$$= \frac{2000}{10000} + \frac{500}{10000} + \frac{80}{10000} + \frac{9}{10000}$$
$$= \frac{2589}{10000}$$

---

### Recurring Decimal Notation

When a digit or sequence of digits repeats infinitely, we use dot notation to represent the recurring part.

*   **Example a) $0,1111111...$**
    *   **Observation:** Only the digit $1$ is repeated.
    *   **Notation:** $0,\dot{1}$

---

### Recurring Decimal Notation

Recurring decimals are numbers that have a repeating pattern of digits. We use a bar (vinculum) over the repeating digits or a dot above the repeating digit to represent this.

#### Examples of Notation
*   $0,121212... = 0,\overline{12}$
*   $0,123123123... = 0,\overline{123}$
*   $0,1141454145... = 0,1\overline{145}$

---

### Exercise: Converting Fractions to Recurring Decimals

To convert a fraction to a decimal, perform long division. If the division results in a repeating pattern, use the recurring decimal notation.

#### a) Convert $\frac{25}{45}$
**Calculation:**
*   $25 \div 45 = 0,555...$
*   The digit $5$ repeats indefinitely.
*   **Result:** $0,\dot{5}$

#### b) Convert $\frac{10}{18}$
**Calculation:**
*   $10 \div 18 = 0,555...$
*   The digit $5$ repeats indefinitely.
*   **Result:** $0,\dot{5}$

#### c) Convert $\frac{7}{33}$
**Calculation:**
*   $7 \div 33 = 0,2121...$
*   The pattern $21$ repeats indefinitely.
*   **Result:** $0,\overline{21}$

#### d) Convert $\frac{2}{3}$
**Calculation:**
*   $2 \div 3 = 0,666...$
*   The digit $6$ repeats indefinitely.
*   **Result:** $0,\dot{6}$

---

<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->

---

### Converting Fractions to Recurring Decimals

To convert a fraction to a decimal, divide the numerator by the denominator. Recurring decimals are indicated by a dot above the repeating digit(s).

**Worked Examples:**

*   **Example e)** $1\frac{3}{11}$
    $$1\frac{3}{11} = 1 + 3\left(\frac{1}{11}\right)$$
    $$= 1 + 3(0,090909...)$$
    $$= 1 + 0,272727...$$
    $$= 1,\overline{27}$$

*   **Example f)** $4\frac{5}{6}$
    $$4\frac{5}{6} = 4 + 5\left(\frac{1}{6}\right)$$
    $$= 4 + 5(0,166666...)$$
    $$= 4 + 0,833333...$$
    $$= 4,8\dot{3}$$

*   **Example g)** $2\frac{1}{9}$
    $$2\frac{1}{9} = 2 + 0,1111111...$$
    $$= 2,\dot{1}$$

---

### 12. Converting Recurring Decimals to Fractions

To convert a recurring decimal to a fraction, use algebraic substitution to eliminate the recurring part.

**a) Convert $0,\dot{5}$ to a fraction:**

Let $x = 0,55555...$
Multiply by $10$ to shift the decimal point:
$$10x = 5,55555...$$
Subtract the original equation ($x$) from the new equation ($10x$):
$$10x - x = (5,55555...) - (0,55555...)$$
$$9x = 5$$
$$x = \frac{5}{9}$$

**b) Convert $0,6\dot{3}$ to a fraction:**

Let $x = 0,63333...$
Multiply by $10$ to isolate the non-recurring part:
$$10x = 6,3333...$$
Multiply by $100$ to shift the decimal point past the first recurring digit:
$$100x = 63,3333...$$
Subtract the two equations:
$$100x - 10x = (63,3333...) - (6,3333...)$$
$$90x = 57$$
$$x = \frac{57}{90}$$

---

### Converting Recurring Decimals to Fractions

To convert a recurring decimal into a fraction, we use algebraic manipulation to eliminate the repeating part.

---

#### Worked Examples

**c) Convert $0,\dot{4}$ to a fraction**

$$x = 0,4444...$$
$$10x = 4,4444...$$
$$10x - x = (4,4444...) - (0,4444...)$$
$$9x = 4$$
$$\therefore x = \frac{4}{9}$$

---

**d) Convert $5,\overline{31}$ to a fraction**

$$x = 5,313131...$$
$$100x = 531,313131...$$
$$100x - x = (531,313131...) - (5,313131...)$$
$$99x = 526$$
$$\therefore x = \frac{526}{99}$$

---

**e) Convert $4,\overline{93}$ to a fraction**

$$x = 4,939393...$$
$$100x = 493,939393...$$
$$100x - x = (493,939393...) - (4,939393...)$$
$$99x = 489$$
$$\therefore x = \frac{489}{99} = \frac{163}{33}$$

---

**f) Convert $3,\overline{93}$ to a fraction**

$$x = 3,939393...$$
$$100x = 393,939393...$$
$$100x - x = (393,939393...) - (3,939393...)$$
$$99x = 390$$
$$\therefore x = \frac{390}{99} = \frac{130}{33}$$

---

# 1.4 Rounding off

## Exercise 1

**1. Round off the following to 3 decimal places:**

a) $12,56637061...$
*   **Solution:** Mark off the required number of decimal places: $12,566|37061...$ The next digit is a $3$, so we round down: $12,566$.

b) $3,31662479...$
*   **Solution:** Mark off the required number of decimal places: $3,316|62479...$ The next digit is a $6$, so we round up: $3,317$.

c) $0,2666666...$
*   **Solution:** Mark off the required number of decimal places: $0,266|6666...$ The next digit is a $6$, so we round up: $0,267$.

d) $1,912931183...$
*   **Solution:** Mark off the required number of decimal places: $1,912|931183...$ The next digit is a $9$, so we round up: $1,913$.

e) $6,32455532...$
*   **Solution:** Mark off the required number of decimal places: $6,324|55532...$ The next digit is a $5$, so we round up: $6,325$.

f) $0,05555555...$
*   **Solution:** Mark off the required number of decimal places: $0,055|5555...$ The next digit is a $5$, so we round up: $0,056$.

---

## Exercise 2

**2. Round off each of the following to the indicated number of decimal places:**

a) $345,04399906$ to 4 decimal places.
*   **Solution:** $345,04399906 \approx 345,0440$

b) $1361,72980445$ to 2 decimal places.
*   **Solution:** $1361,72980445 \approx 1361,73$

c) $728,00905239$ to 6 decimal places.
*   **Solution:** $728,00905239 \approx 728,009052$

d) $\frac{1}{27}$ to 4 decimal places.
*   **Solution:** We first write the fraction as a decimal and then we can round off.
    $$\frac{1}{27} = 0,037037...$$
    $$\approx 0,0370$$

e) $\frac{45}{99}$ to 5 decimal places.
*   **Solution:** (Note: The provided image cuts off before the final calculation for this item).

### Rounding Fractions to Decimal Places

To round a fraction to a specific number of decimal places, first convert the fraction into its decimal form, then round the result.

**Example:**
Round $\frac{1}{12}$ to 2 decimal places.

**Solution:**
1. Convert to decimal: $\frac{1}{12} = 0,08333...$
2. Round to 2 decimal places: $\approx 0,08$

---

### Geometry: Area Calculations

<!-- DIAGRAM_PLACEHOLDER: diagram -->

#### a) Calculate the area of $ABDE$ to 2 decimal places.
$ABDE$ is a square with side length $\pi$.
The formula for the area of a square is $A = l^2$.

$$A = l^2$$
$$A = \pi^2$$
$$A = 9,86904...$$
$$A \approx 9,87$$

#### b) Calculate the area of $BCD$ to 2 decimal places.
$BCD$ is a right-angled triangle with base $\pi$ and height $\pi$.
The formula for the area of a triangle is $A = \frac{1}{2}bh$.

$$A = \frac{1}{2}bh$$
$$A = \frac{1}{2}(\pi)(\pi) = \frac{1}{2}\pi^2$$
$$A = 4,934802...$$
$$A \approx 4,93$$

#### c) Calculate the area of $ABCDE$.
The area of the composite shape $ABCDE$ is the sum of the areas of $ABDE$ and $BCD$.

$$A = 9,87 + 4,93$$
$$A \approx 14,80$$

#### d) Without rounding off, what is the area of $ABCDE$?
To find the exact area without intermediate rounding, sum the expressions:

$$A = \pi^2 + \frac{1}{2}\pi^2$$
$$A = \frac{3}{2}\pi^2$$

---

### Area Calculation Example
The area of a composite shape ($A_{ABCDE}$) is calculated by summing the areas of its components:
$$A_{ABCDE} = A_{ABDE} + A_{BCD}$$
$$= l^2 + \frac{1}{2}bh$$
$$= \pi^2 + \frac{1}{2}\pi^2$$
$$= 14,8044...$$

---

### 4. Financial Calculation: Compound Interest
**Given:** $i = \frac{r}{600}$; $r = 7,4$; $n = 96$; $P = 200\,000$

**a) Calculate $i$ correct to 2 decimal places.**
$$i = \frac{r}{600}$$
$$i = \frac{7,4}{600}$$
$$i = 0,01233...$$
$$i \approx 0,01$$

**b) Using your answer from (a), calculate $A$ in $A = P(1 + i)^n$.**
$$A = P(1 + i)^n$$
$$A = 200\,000(1 + 0,01)^{96}$$
$$A = 519\,854,59$$

**c) Calculate $A$ without rounding off your answer in (a), compare this answer with your answer in (b).**
$$A = P(1 + i)^n$$
$$A = 200\,000\left(1 + \frac{7,4}{600}\right)^{96}$$
$$A = 648\,768,22$$

*Note: There is a $128\,913,63$ difference between the answer in (b) and the one calculated without rounding until the final step.*

---

### 5. Practical Application: Rounding Up
**Problem:** If it takes 1 person to carry 3 boxes, how many people are needed to carry 31 boxes?

**Solution:**
Divide the total number of boxes by the capacity per person:
$$\frac{31}{3} = 10,3333...$$
Since we cannot have $0,333$ of a person, we must round up to the nearest whole number to ensure all boxes are carried.
**Result:** 11 people are needed.

---

### 6. Practical Application: Currency Rounding
**Problem:** If 7 tickets cost R 35,20, how much does one ticket cost?

**Solution:**
Divide the total cost by the number of tickets:
$$\frac{35,20}{7} = 5,028571429$$
Money should be rounded off to 2 decimal places.
**Result:** One ticket costs R 5,03.

---

# 1.5 Estimating Surds

## Exercise 1

### 1. Determine between which two consecutive integers the following numbers lie, without using a calculator:

| Question | Solution | Reasoning |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| a) $\sqrt{18}$ | 4 and 5 | $4^2 = 16$ and $5^2 = 25$ |
| b) $\sqrt{29}$ | 5 and 6 | $5^2 = 25$ and $6^2 = 36$ |
| c) $\sqrt[3]{5}$ | 1 and 2 | $1^3 = 1$ and $2^3 = 8$ |
| d) $\sqrt[3]{79}$ | 4 and 5 | $4^3 = 64$ and $5^3 = 125$ |
| e) $\sqrt{155}$ | 12 and 13 | $12^2 = 144$ and $13^2 = 169$ |
| f) $\sqrt{57}$ | 7 and 8 | $7^2 = 49$ and $8^2 = 64$ |
| g) $\sqrt{71}$ | 8 and 9 | $8^2 = 64$ and $9^2 = 81$ |
| h) $\sqrt[3]{123}$ | 4 and 5 | $4^3 = 64$ and $5^3 = 125$ |
| i) $\sqrt[3]{90}$ | 4 and 5 | $4^3 = 64$ and $5^3 = 125$ |
| j) $\sqrt[3]{81}$ | 4 and 5 | $4^3 = 64$ and $5^3 = 125$ |

---

### 2. Estimate the following surds to the nearest 1 decimal place, without using a calculator:

**a) $\sqrt{10}$**
*   **Reasoning:** Since $3^2 = 9$ and $4^2 = 16$, $\sqrt{10}$ must lie between 3 and 4.
*   **Observation:** 10 is closer to 9 than to 16.
*   **Estimate:** 3,1 or 3,2.

**b) $\sqrt{82}$**
*   **Reasoning:** Since $9^2 = 81$ and $10^2 = 100$, $\sqrt{82}$ must lie between 9 and 10.
*   **Observation:** 82 is closer to 81 than to 100.
*   **Estimate:** 9,1.

**c) $\sqrt{15}$**
*   **Reasoning:** Since $3^2 = 9$ and $4^2 = 16$, $\sqrt{15}$ must lie between 3 and 4.
*   **Observation:** 15 is closer to 16 than to 9.
*   **Estimate:** 3,9.

**d) $\sqrt{90}$**
*   *(Note: Calculation required based on method above)*
*   **Reasoning:** Since $9^2 = 81$ and $10^2 = 100$, $\sqrt{90}$ must lie between 9 and 10.
*   **Observation:** 90 is roughly halfway between 81 and 100.
*   **Estimate:** 9,5.

---

### Estimating and Ordering Real Numbers

**Worked Example: Ranking Numbers**

**Problem:** Consider the following list of numbers:
$\frac{27}{7} ; \sqrt{19} ; 2\pi ; 0,45 ; 0,\overline{45} ; -\sqrt{\frac{9}{4}} ; 6 ; -\sqrt{8} ; \sqrt{51}$
Without using a calculator, rank all the numbers in ascending order.

**Solution Strategy:**
1.  **Negative numbers:** Remember that negative numbers are always smaller than positive numbers.
2.  **Fractions:** Convert fractions to decimals to assist with estimation.
3.  **Surds:** Estimate which two integers the surd lies between.

**Step-by-step Estimation:**
*   $\frac{27}{7} \approx 3,857$
*   $\sqrt{19}$: Since $4^2 = 16$ and $5^2 = 25$, $\sqrt{19}$ lies between $4$ and $5$.
*   $2\pi \approx 2 \times 3,14 = 6,28$
*   $-\sqrt{\frac{9}{4}} = -\frac{3}{2} = -1,5$
*   $-\sqrt{8}$: Since $2^2 = 4$ and $3^2 = 9$, $-\sqrt{8}$ lies between $-2$ and $-3$.
*   $\sqrt{51}$: Since $7^2 = 49$ and $8^2 = 64$, $\sqrt{51}$ lies between $7$ and $8$.
*   Note: $0,45 < 0,\overline{45}$

**Final Ascending Order:**
$$-\sqrt{8} ; -\sqrt{\frac{9}{4}} ; 0,45 ; 0,\overline{45} ; \frac{27}{7} ; \sqrt{19} ; 6 ; 2\pi ; \sqrt{51}$$

---

**Solution:**

### Algebraic Expressions: Binomial Expansion Exercises

This section focuses on the expansion of binomial products. Use the distributive property (often referred to as the FOIL method) to multiply each term in the first bracket by each term in the second bracket, then simplify by combining like terms.

---

#### Worked Examples

**c) $(2 - t)(1 - 2t)$**
$$(2 - t)(1 - 2t) = 2 - 4t - t + 2t^2$$
$$= 2t^2 - 5t + 2$$

**d) $(x - 4)(x + 4)$**
$$(x - 4)(x + 4) = x^2 + 4x - 4x - 16$$
$$= x^2 - 16$$

**e) $-(4 - x)(x + 4)$**
$$-(4 - x)(x + 4) = -(4x + 16 - x^2 - 4x)$$
$$= -(16 - x^2)$$
$$= -16 + x^2$$
$$= x^2 - 16$$

**f) $-(a + b)(b - a)$**
$$-(a + b)(b - a) = (a + b)(a - b)$$
$$= a^2 + ba - ba - b^2$$
$$= a^2 - b^2$$

**g) $(2p + 9)(3p + 1)$**
$$(2p + 9)(3p + 1) = 6p^2 + 2p + 27p + 9$$
$$= 6p^2 + 29p + 9$$

**h) $(3k - 2)(k + 6)$**
$$(3k - 2)(k + 6) = 3k^2 + 18k - 2k - 12$$
$$= 3k^2 + 16k - 12$$

**i) $(s + 6)^2$**
$$(s + 6)^2 = (s + 6)(s + 6)$$
$$= s^2 + 6s + 6s + 36$$
$$= s^2 + 12s + 36$$

**j) $-(7 - x)(7 + x)$**
*(Note: This follows the difference of squares pattern)*
$$-(7 - x)(7 + x) = -(49 + 7x - 7x - x^2)$$
$$= -(49 - x^2)$$
$$= -49 + x^2$$
$$= x^2 - 49$$

---

### Algebraic Products: Worked Examples

This section provides step-by-step solutions for expanding binomial products.

---

#### Example: Expansion of $-(7 - x)(7 + x)$
$$
\begin{aligned}
-(7 - x)(7 + x) &= -(49 + 7x - 7x - x^2) \\
&= -(49 - x^2) \\
&= x^2 - 49
\end{aligned}
$$

#### k) $(3x - 1)(3x + 1)$
**Solution:**
$$
\begin{aligned}
(3x - 1)(3x + 1) &= 9x^2 + 3x - 3x - 1 \\
&= 9x^2 - 1
\end{aligned}
$$

#### l) $(7k + 2)(3 - 2k)$
**Solution:**
$$
\begin{aligned}
(7k + 2)(3 - 2k) &= 21k - 14k^2 + 6 - 4k \\
&= -14k^2 + 17k + 6
\end{aligned}
$$

#### m) $(1 - 4x)^2$
**Solution:**
$$
\begin{aligned}
(1 - 4x)^2 &= (1 - 4x)(1 - 4x) \\
&= 1 - 4x - 4x + 16x^2 \\
&= 16x^2 - 8x + 1
\end{aligned}
$$

#### n) $(-3 - y)(5 - y)$
**Solution:**
$$
\begin{aligned}
(-3 - y)(5 - y) &= -15 + 3y - 5y + y^2 \\
&= y^2 - 2y - 15
\end{aligned}
$$

#### o) $(8 - x)(8 + x)$
**Solution:**
$$
\begin{aligned}
(8 - x)(8 + x) &= 64 + 8x - 8x - x^2 \\
&= -x^2 + 64
\end{aligned}
$$

#### p) $(9 + x)^2$
**Solution:**
$$
\begin{aligned}
(9 + x)^2 &= (9 + x)(9 + x) \\
&= 81 + 9x + 9x + x^2 \\
&= x^2 + 18x + 81
\end{aligned}
$$

#### q) $(-7y + 11)(-12y + 3)$
**Solution:**
$$
\begin{aligned}
(-7y + 11)(-12y + 3) &= 84y^2 - 21y - 132y + 33 \\
&= 84y^2 - 153y + 33
\end{aligned}
$$

#### r) $(g - 5)^2$
**Solution:**
$$
\begin{aligned}
(g - 5)^2 &= (g - 5)(g - 5) \\
&= g^2 - 5g - 5g + 25 \\
&= g^2 - 10g + 25
\end{aligned}
$$

---

### Algebraic Expressions: Expansion of Binomials

This section focuses on the expansion of algebraic products, specifically binomials. To expand these expressions, use the distributive property (often referred to as the FOIL method: First, Outer, Inner, Last).

#### Worked Examples

**s) Expand $(d + 9)^2$**
$$(d + 9)^2 = (d + 9)(d + 9)$$
$$= d^2 + 9d + 9d + 81$$
$$= d^2 + 18d + 81$$

**t) Expand $(6d + 7)(6d - 7)$**
$$(6d + 7)(6d - 7) = 36d^2 - 42d + 42d - 49$$
$$= 36d^2 - 49$$

**u) Expand $(5z + 1)(5z - 1)$**
$$(5z + 1)(5z - 1) = 25z^2 - 5z + 5z - 1$$
$$= 25z^2 - 1$$

**v) Expand $(1 - 3h)(1 + 3h)$**
$$(1 - 3h)(1 + 3h) = 1 + 3h - 3h - 9h^2$$
$$= 1 - 9h^2$$

**w) Expand $(2p + 3)(2p + 2)$**
$$(2p + 3)(2p + 2) = 4p^2 + 4p + 6p + 6$$
$$= 4p^2 + 10p + 6$$

**x) Expand $(8a + 4)(a + 7)$**
$$(8a + 4)(a + 7) = 8a^2 + 56a + 4a + 28$$
$$= 8a^2 + 60a + 28$$

**y) Expand $(5r + 4)(2r + 4)$**
$$(5r + 4)(2r + 4) = 10r^2 + 20r + 8r + 16$$
$$= 10r^2 + 28r + 16$$

**z) Expand $(w + 1)(w - 1)$**
$$(w + 1)(w - 1) = w^2 + w - w - 1$$
$$= w^2 - 1$$

---
